and names the collapse of names as the error itself, which is a third witness to the distinction.

---

## EXCURSUS III · WHAT THE FOUR PRESERVED ARAMAIC ADDRESSES DO

The earliest Gospel stops translating four times and keeps the Aramaic in the speaker's mouth,
each glossed for the Greek reader as a foreign thing preserved.

*Talitha koum*, at 5:41, to a dead child.

*Ephphatha*, at 7:34, to a closed ear.

*Abba*, at 14:36, in the garden.

*Elōi Elōi lema sabachthani*, at 15:34, from the cross.

**The pattern is that all four are addresses**, and each is at a moment where a barrier is in
question: death, deafness, a request that a cup pass, and abandonment.

Chapter 59 used the preservation to argue against the Hellenisation critique landing on this text:
**a text being Hellenised does not keep the source language at the moments of address and translate
it in an aside.**

The further observation, offered at structural grade, is that the four cluster at the points where
the Gospel's own narrative is most concerned with what does and does not get through, which is the
same concern Books II and IX found the whole material organised around.

---

## EXCURSUS IV · THE UNANSWERED-CRY CLAUSE

1 Samuel 8:18 deserves separate treatment because it is the single most consequential sentence in
the concession narrative and is almost always read as a threat.

*U-zeʿaqtem ba-yom ha-huʾ mi-lifney malkekhem ʾasher beḥartem lakhem, we-loʾ yaʿaneh YHWH ʾetkhem
ba-yom ha-huʾ.*

**Why it is a term and not a threat.** A warning says: this will go badly. A term says: when it
goes badly, here is what the remedy situation will be. This clause specifies the remedy situation.

**Two features make it contractual.** *ʾAsher beḥartem lakhem*, whom you have chosen for
yourselves, locates the choice with the choosing party in advance. And the consequence is stated
with precision: not punishment, not abandonment, but **no answer to this particular cry**.

**What it suspends.** The corpus's ordinary pattern, established across Judges, is
cry-and-deliverance: the people cry out and a deliverer is raised. This clause suspends that
pattern **for this cause**.

**The consequence for a study of the office.** A long history of failed and oppressive occupants,
with no rescue arriving from that direction, has an answer sitting in the founding transaction:
the ordinary intervention was suspended by the terms of the grant, in advance, in writing, and the
people renewed the demand after hearing it.

---

## EXCURSUS V · ON THE OBADIAH FINDING

Chapter 54 recorded a finding against this work's own register, and it is repeated here because
findings against the instrument belong where a reader can find them.

**Obadiah**, *ʿal ha-bayit*, hid a hundred prophets in two caves, fifty in each, and supplied them
with bread and water while continuing to run the king's establishment, at 1 Kings 18:4.

**That is the funding operation of the entire resistance**, executed by a salaried royal official
at mortal risk, and it is what made the guild survive to transmit anything.

He is nameable. Under the register's own carrier law, where the carrier is nameable the carrier is
seated. **He is not seated.**

Audit symmetry: an instrument that finds unseated carriers in the material it reads should find
them in its own, and this one did, and records it rather than absorbing it.

The corpus also preserves his fear, at length and from his own side, at 1 Kings 18:9-14. **The cost
of association, recorded from the associate's side**, is a rarer thing in this literature than the
cost recorded from the principal's, and it is worth noticing that this corpus kept it.

---

## EXCURSUS VI · THE THIRTEENTH GATE THAT DOES NOT EXIST

A note on why the analytic apparatus this work draws on has the counts it has, offered because a
reader may reasonably ask whether the numbers are fitted.

The verification apparatus behind this study runs a twelve-gate cascade and an eight-gate one, and
the counts are not chosen.

**Twelve** is the number of ordered pairs of distinct vertices in a four-vertex complete graph, and
four vertices is the minimum closing a three-axis structure, which Chapter 14 established.

**Eight** is the number of sign-patterns over three independent axes, 2³, which is what remains when
orientation is deleted.

Both are forced by the same fact: **the residence has three axes**, which is forced in turn by the
classification of the real division algebras, since the only associative one with plural imaginary
axes has exactly three.

The consequence is that there is no thirteenth gate and no ninth, and there could not be. The
counts are read off a structure rather than fitted to a case, and a reader who declines the whole
apparatus loses this excursus and nothing else in the book, since no finding in the twelve books
rests on a gate count.

**And the same discipline caught a fitted count elsewhere.** A five-gate protocol in the same
apparatus was at one point inflated toward seven to mirror a sibling protocol's count, and the
inflation was identified and reversed on the ground that the two protocols answer different
admission questions and their counts are forced by different objects. That correction is recorded
in the apparatus's own record and is repeated here because a study that reports its instrument's
numbers owes a reader the instrument's own error record.

---

## EXCURSUS VII · THE HARDER CASE AGAINST FATHERLESSNESS

An argument in this area is rarely met at its strongest and is worth isolating.

**The inference under examination.** A birth without a human father implies divine sonship.

**The counter-case supplied by one corpus.** Adam AS had **neither father nor mother**, and nobody
has ever concluded divine sonship from that.

**Why this is the right form of objection.** It does not deny the datum or reinterpret the birth.
It supplies a stronger instance of the same feature and observes that the tradition running the
inference does not run it there.

If fatherlessness were the ground, then a complete absence of both parents would be **stronger**
ground, and the inference is not made. So fatherlessness is not the ground, and something else is
doing the work.

**What the corpus supplies instead.** The mechanism: *kun fa-yakūn*, Be, and it is, at Q 2:117 and
Q 19:35, given in the clause immediately after the refusal in both places.

**The structural point Chapter 67 draws from this.** *Nothing is taken and left empty.* Each refusal
in that corpus is accompanied by the correct form supplied in the same passage: the equation
refused and written affirmatively elsewhere with the office correctly filled; the count barred in
the verse that supplies three designations for one referent; and the begetting denied with the
mechanism named.

That is a distinctive method and it is worth naming: **a corpus that refuses a construction and
supplies its replacement is doing something different from a corpus that merely denies.**

---

## EXCURSUS VIII · THE COVENANT OF SALT

*Echete en heautois hala*, have salt in yourselves, at Mark 9:50, closes the hardest chapter of
discipleship in the earliest Gospel and has puzzled readers.

**Salt is the one thing in the ancient kitchen a man cannot grow.**

That is the point, and the Torah had already made it law.

**Numbers 18:19**, *berit melaḥ ʿolam*, a covenant of salt forever, is a **stipend**: the priests
eat what they did not produce, and the covenant's token is the substance that marks provision from
elsewhere.

And the Persian court's idiom, *we eat the palace's salt*, means we are on the king's provision.

**A covenant sealed in salt is a declaration of dependence built into the material itself.**

The parallel corpus carries the same economy without the token: every joining of hearts is His
purchase, not yours, and what you were given to keep, you were given.

**Why this belongs in a study of the office.** Chapter 69 read it beside the cup, and the pair
carries the same content: an office whose covenant tokens are a substance no one can produce and a
blood no one is to drink is an office of dependence stated in the material rather than in a
doctrine about it.

---

## EXCURSUS IX · THE THREE-CORPUS TRAJECTORY OF THE COVENANT TOKEN

The observation of Chapter 69, isolated and stated at its own grade.

**Sinai.** *Hinneh dam ha-berit*, behold the blood of the covenant, at Exodus 24:8, where the blood
is **thrown upon the people and never drunk**. Applied externally.

**The supper.** *Touto estin to haima mou tēs diathēkēs*, this is my blood of the covenant, at Mark
14:24, quoting Sinai's phrase, in a cup at a meal.

**And the correction of a community's own sacrifice.** *Lan yanāla Allāha luḥūmuhā wa-lā dimāʾuhā
wa-lākin yanāluhu al-taqwā minkum*, neither their flesh nor their blood reaches Allah ﷻ, but the
reverence from you reaches Him, at Q 22:37.

**Blood applied, then blood in a cup, then the word alone.**

The covenant token is progressively interiorised across three scriptures until nothing ascends but
*taqwā*.

**The grade.** This is a structural observation across three corpora, offered as a reading of a
sequence rather than as any corpus's claim about the others. No line of derivation is asserted, and
a reader who takes the three as independent developments loses nothing else in the book.

**What makes it worth stating.** The middle term is the one everything turns on. A reader who has
only the first and third sees a contrast. A reader who has all three sees a trajectory, and the
Gospel's cup stands at its middle rather than at either end.

---

## EXCURSUS X · THE OPEN GROUND

A small matter with an unexpectedly precise doctrinal counterpart.

**The scene.** A woman anoints him unbidden, at Mark 14:3-9. The objectors **price her act**: this
ointment might have been sold for three hundred denarii and given to the poor.

**The response.** *Kalon ergon ērgasato en emoi*, she has worked a good work on me. And the act is
bound to the proclamation: wherever the good news is preached in the whole world, what she has done
shall be told.

**The structure.** The objectors legislate. The master receives.

**And the parallel corpus names the category.** *ʿAfā Allāhu ʿanhā*, things Allah ﷻ has left alone,
at Q 5:101: an **unlegislated space** that the legalists rush to fill and that had been deliberately
left open.

The verse's own context is a warning against asking about matters that, if disclosed, would burden
the asker, and the phrase names what has been left unruled as a mercy rather than as an oversight.

**Why the pair is worth recording.** Two corpora, one structure: an act performed in a space no
rule covers, objectors who supply a rule, and a text that declines the supply and names the space
as deliberately open.

The office reading is direct. An office that legislates everything has no open ground, and both
corpora describe theirs as having some.

---

## EXCURSUS XI · MARRIAGE IN THE RISING

A place where two corpora look opposed and turn out to be two halves of one sentence.

**Mark 12:25.** *Hotan gar ek nekrōn anastōsin, oute gamousin oute gamizontai.* When they rise from
the dead they neither marry nor are given in marriage.

Read alone this is standardly taken as a denial of relation in the resurrection.

**But what is denied is a transaction.** *Gamousin* and *gamizontai* are the active and the
causative of the marrying verb: to marry, and to give in marriage. Both are **human acts**, and
the second is the arranging party's.

**And the parallel corpus never once puts the wedding verb in creaturely hands in the Garden.** It
says *zawwajnāhum*, We paired them, at Q 44:54 and Q 52:20, with **He as the only subject the verb
ever takes there.**

**The two together.** One text removes the contract from the creatures. The other assigns the
pairing to the Lord.

**Together they say a single thing: the transacting ends, the gift remains.**

The grade is structural and the observation is checkable: the Greek verbs are transactional and the
Arabic verb's subject in those verses is uniform. A reader who checks and finds otherwise has
grounds to reject the reading.

---

## EXCURSUS XII · THE FOUR TRANSFERS AND THE FOUR WITHHELD ORIGINS, SIDE BY SIDE

Chapters 38 and 40 read two habits that look opposite and are complementary. Setting them in one
table makes the complementarity visible.

| Named and kept | Withheld entirely |
|---|---|
| A priestly order, from Melchizedek, Gen 14:18-20, permanent at Ps 110:4 | Melchizedek's own genealogy: no patronymic, no origin, no exit |
| A divine title, *ʾEl ʿElyon qoneh shamayim wa-ʾareṣ*, spoken by that priest at 14:19, taken onto Abram's oath at 14:22 with the Name appended | Tamar's ancestry, in a chapter that supplies her narrative, her judgment scene, and her twins |
| An administrative procedure, the delegated judiciary of Exod 18:13-23, adopted in full at 18:24 | The Cushite woman at Num 12:1: no name, no father, no narrative, mentioned once |
| The sanctuary's ground, *goren ʾOrnan ha-Yevusi*, 2 Chr 3:1, bought and not seized, 2 Sam 24:24 | Job's frame: *ʾish hayah be-ʾereṣ ʿUṣ*, no Israelite genealogy, no covenantal setting |

**Where the corpus names, it names precisely and at cost**, preserving material a tidier tradition
would have removed.

**Where the corpus withholds, it withholds completely**, leaving no partial information from which
an inference could be reconstructed.

**Both are forms of not-editing.** The first keeps what is awkward; the second declines to supply
what is absent. Neither is the behaviour of a tradition constructing a case.

One further observation at low grade. The withholdings cluster at load-bearing junctures: the
Davidic line's head, the priestly order the royal psalm confers, a marriage that occasioned a
rebuke, and the book carrying the sharpest ruling on the image the central promise uses.

**Four instances is a small number and the clustering could be coincidence**, and no systematic
sweep has been run to establish that withholdings are more frequent at load-bearing points than
elsewhere. It is offered as an observation and not as a finding.

---

## EXCURSUS XIII · THE INSTITUTIONAL PATTERN BEHIND THE SECOND MANUFACTURE

Chapter 81 identified the manufactured absence and named its conditions. The instances are gathered
here because the pattern is checkable and the checking is the point.

**The claim.** The second manufacture recurs at every consolidation in the lineage and under the
same conditions: **a credentialed class under external pressure adopting a philosophical guarantee
that the presence cannot be seized.**

**Instance one.** The second custody imported divine simplicity, impassibility, and incorporeality
from Middle Platonism and Aristotle, **none of which appear in its own founding documents**, and
condemned the New Prophecy while closing the canon.

**Instance two, and it is the sharpest**, because there the abstract position arrived **with an
inquisition attached**. From 833 the Abbasid caliphs enforced the negation of the attributes by
state examination in the Miḥna, and Ahmad ibn Hanbal was flogged and imprisoned for refusing it.

**A position requiring a caliph's inquisition to install had, whatever the sincerity of its
holders, a political economy.**

**Instance three.** Three centuries later Maimonides made incorporeality a creedal article and
placed corporealists outside the faith, and the protest is recorded at the moment of hardening by
Rabad of Posquières, who objected that greater and better men than he had believed otherwise.

**The pattern's checkable form.** *The plain sense is the older layer and the apparatus built to
manage it is the newer institution.*

**And the test.** *Nobody funds a library to defend a reading that was never in doubt.*

The presence of a substantial defensive apparatus is evidence that the position it defends was
contested, which is evidence that it was not the received reading, which is what the pattern
claims.

**The fence.** This says nothing about whether any of the three positions is true. A position can
be true and late, and a plain sense can be old and wrong. What the pattern establishes is the
**order**, and the order is the datum.

---

## EXCURSUS XIV · WHAT WOULD CHANGE THE FINDINGS

A study that states no falsifiers is a description. These are the conditions under which each
principal finding of this work would move.

**Book II's terminus finding.** Falsified by a concordance showing the *nwḥ*, *menuḥah*, and *šqṭ*
terminal-position sites are **systematically figure-indexed**, requiring a royal or eschatological
agent in the clause or its immediate pericope. That concordance has not been run and Book II is
capped at the debt.

**Book II's receiver-deficit finding.** Falsified by a single passage in the Hebrew corpus
attributing the non-entry to an absent, withheld, delayed, or reserved terminus. This work found
none across five passages in three books; one would move the finding, and a pattern of them would
end it.

**Book V's ladder.** Weakened by showing that the rungs do not stand in the described
content-relation. Unaffected by any re-dating, by construction.

**Book VI's recipient-invariant.** Falsified by a corpus that carries the title and does **not**
write its bearer as a recipient at the point of maximum exaltation. Six corpora were surveyed and
none does; a seventh that did would scope the finding.

**Book X's exclusion prediction.** Falsified by exclusions that seat their author routinely
appearing with **no citation drawn from the excluded register**, the positive warrant coming from a
register the exclusion did not touch. One ambiguous case is in hand.

**Book XI's seal reading.** Falsified by a ninth occurrence of the root, or by showing the
verbal-nominal division does not hold across the eight, or by a nominal use that blocks rather than
certifies.

**Book XII's disqualification architecture.** Falsified by **a claimant who announces and
succeeds**. Chapter 99 states that this falsifier is thin, since it has not obtained in thirteen
centuries and a prediction that the future resembles the past is cheap.

**And the office frame itself.** Falsified by showing that the corpora name an occupant rather than
describing a position: that is, by a passage in which a corpus states the identity of the
office-holder in a way that admits no alternative filling. Six corpora were read and none does, and
one that did would end this work's central frame rather than qualify it.

---

# PART THREE · ELEVEN APPENDICES

*Reference apparatus. Tables, ledgers, and the record of what this work corrected in itself.*

---

## APPENDIX A · THE OFFICE FRAME IN ONE PAGE

For a reader who wants the structure without the argument.

**A closure position exists wherever a manifold is read as one.** It is single, it is not a member
of what it closes, and it is invariant under variation of the members. Four clauses, each
separately deniable.

**A position and its occupant are distinct.** No fact about one is a fact about the other. Four
moves are barred: the occupant does not acquire the position's properties by occupying it; the
position does not acquire the occupant's; occupancy is not identity even when total; and vacancy is
not abolition.

**An exclusion produces a vacancy and never an appointment.** Not-Authority(A) entails Authority(B)
for no B. A party that both excludes and requires the seat runs a debt payable only in counterfeit,
whose predicted form is a citation drawn from the excluded register.

**Empty carries two senses.** Empty-of-climbers carries a theorem: no ascending procedure arrives
at the position. Empty-of-an-occupant is proven nowhere. Neither entails the other, and the
inference from the first to the second is the void-claim inversion.

**Position stable, occupant variable.** Four instances across four books: the closure position
under counting; the throne occupied by act; the office filled and refilled; and the conferred
throne.

---

---

## APPENDIX C · THE EVIDENCE TIERS

Every non-trivial claim in this work carries a tier. The tiers, and what each licenses.

| Tier | What it is | What it licenses |
|---|---|---|
| Primary text | The corpus itself, cited and read | The strongest available warrant in this work |
| Philological | A root, lemma, construction, or distribution across a corpus | Checkable by anyone with the text and a concordance |
| Structural | A pattern across sites, this work's reading rather than a corpus's word | A reading a reader may decline |
| Corroboration | A witness that agrees | Load-bearing on nothing; removable without loss |
| Contested | Evidence genuinely forks | Both branches stated, neither smoothed |
| Demoted | Rests on a gapped corpus | Reported, never adjudicated |

**The tiers matter most where they are lowest.** A structural reading a reader declines costs that
reader one paragraph. A structural reading presented as a philological finding costs the reader the
ability to tell which is which.

---

## APPENDIX D · THE THREE DECLARED GAPS

| Gap | What is absent | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Rabbinic | No primary Mishnah, Talmud, Midrash, or Targum corpus in hand | Rabbinic claims reported at secondary tier, never adjudicated |
| Patristic | No primary Ante-Nicene or conciliar corpus in hand | Patristic claims reported at secondary tier; the conciliar record described, never quoted as primary |
| Shiʿi | No primary Shiʿi hadith or Imami corpus in hand | Claims beyond the disqualification card's own citations are demoted |

**What is not a gap.** The Hebrew Bible, the Gospels, and the Qurʾān are all present as primary
text through spine papers each built scripture-internally. The office's four-corpus spine is fully
sourced.

**The gaps are in the interpretive traditions that grew around three of the four, not in the
scriptures.**

**And no argument in the twelve books turns on a gapped source**, because an argument that did
would be one this work cannot support.

## APPENDIX E · THE TEN PAIRS

The corpus's habit of preserving its own contradictions, from Chapter 34, reproduced for reference.

| Barred or subordinated | Seated or restored |
|---|---|
| Deut 23:4-7, the Moabite and Ammonite barred to the tenth generation | Ruth 4:18-22, the line sealed through Ruth *ha-Moʾaviyyah*; 1 Kgs 14:21, Naʿamah *ha-ʿAmmonit* |
| Jer 22:24-30, the signet plucked from Coniah, *ʿariri* | Hag 2:23, the signet restored to Zerubbabel, his grandson at 1 Chr 3:17 |
| Isa 49:3, the servant named Israel | Isa 49:6, the servant distinguished from the tribes, four verses later |
| Jer 7:22, with 1 Sam 15:22, Hos 6:6, Isa 1:11 | Ezek 40-48, the offerings restored, the prince's six lambs at 46:4 |
| Judg 8:23 and 1 Sam 8:7, kingship refused and named rejection | 1 Chr 29:23, the king seated on the throne of the LORD |
| 1 Sam 8:11-18, the itemised prosecution | Ps 45:7, the king's throne addressed as *ʾelohim* |
| Num 25:12-13 and Ezek 44:15, a perpetual Aaronide priesthood and its exclusion clause | Ps 110:4, a perpetual priesthood on a non-Aaronide, pre-Israelite order |
| Deut 7:1-3 and Exod 34:15-16, *loʾ titḥatten bam* | The ancestry seated through those nations in seven instances |
| Job 14:8, the stump dies in the dust | Isa 11:1, the shoot comes from the stump |
| Ps 23:1, the king says *YHWH roʿi*; 2 Sam 5:2 sets him over *ʿammi* | Ezek 34:24, *ʿavdi Dawid nasiʾ* while God remains *le-ʾelohim* |

**A tradition operating by deletion does not preserve its own prosecution ten times.**

## APPENDIX F · THE SIX CORPORA AND THE RECIPIENT GRAMMAR

Book VI's survey in one table. The column that does not vary is the last.

| Corpus | The figure's exaltation | The grammar of what he has |
|---|---|---|
| Hebrew Bible, Ezekiel | None. A mortal addressed at commission, 90+ vocatives | Address received; instruction given |
| Hebrew Bible, Daniel 7 | Maximum: clouds, universal service, everlasting dominion | *Yəhīv*, was given; brought near by others; the Giver named two verses earlier |
| Pseudepigrapha, Parables | Pre-existent, hidden, enthroned as judge | Chosen before creation; and identified as a man who was taken |
| Qumran | Plural anointed; a heavenly Melchizedek; works listed | Awaited; the anointing conferred; the holder unnamed at 4Q521 |
| New Testament, Synoptic | Seated at the right hand, coming with clouds | Both source verses are conferral texts; *exousia* with a stated domain; below a line at Mk 13:32 |
| Gnostic | Pre-cosmic emanation | **The reversal**: the grammar breaks |
| Qurʾān | Messiah 11×, Word, Spirit, knowledge of the Hour | *Bi-idhni Allāh* on every work; *ʿabd* from the cradle; and *basharun mithlukum* restated against the reversal |

**The exaltation varies from a mortal on the ground to a pre-cosmic principle. The receiving does
not vary, except at the one corpus that broke it, and a later corpus names the break.**

Chapter 47's catalogue and Chapter 98's, combined, with what each entry is evidence of.

**Placed in the office by a community:**

| Figure | Placed by | Basis |
|---|---|---|

| David | The royal anchor; the lineage claim | Ps 110:1, on the flattened Greek |

**Explicit historical claimants, all failed:**

| Claimant | Century | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya | Late 7th | Failed |
| Ibn Tūmart | 12th | Founded a dynasty; the claim did not hold |
| Sayyid Muḥammad Jaunpūrī | Late 15th | Failed |
| Muḥammad Aḥmad | 1880s | Substantial consolidation; died of typhus at the height; successor lost it in two decades |
| The Bābī claimant | 19th | Failed |
| Muḥammad bin ʿAbd Allāh al-Qaḥṭānī | 1979 | Dead in the suppression within weeks |

**What the record supports.** Two readings, and it does not distinguish them: a structural
constraint in the office, or an empty category. Both predict failure. Only a success would
separate them.

## APPENDIX H · THE CORRECTIONS THIS WORK CARRIES

Findings that died on the evidence are recorded rather than dropped, because a reader who cannot
see the corrections cannot calibrate what remains.

**Partial removability, dead.** An earlier form of the terminus reading held that removability was
partial and that the ingathering's terminus required the messianic frame to carry it. **Ezekiel
37:14 killed it**: the ingathering's own terminal clause is *we-hinnaḥti*, in the settling-root,
and it survives the strip with the rest. Removability is **full**.

**The removability asymmetry, dead.** An asymmetry claimed between two disqualification
architectures rested on the partial reading and died with it. Both frames are fully removable.

**The fusion reading at Zechariah 6:13, withdrawn.** An earlier form read the verse as fusing two
offices in one person. *Beyn shenehem* requires **two parties** and the construction does not
support an internal reading. The two-anointed structure is stated positively instead, and the
withdrawal is grammatical rather than theological.

**The clean observability inversion, scoped.** A second difference claimed in the same comparison
is now scoped rather than asserted.

**A fitted count, reversed.** In the analytic apparatus this work draws on, a five-gate protocol
was at one point inflated toward seven to mirror a sibling's count. The inflation was identified
and reversed on the ground that the two protocols answer different admission questions and their
counts are forced by different objects.

**An overstatement by omission at Chapter 82, repaired.** The chapter reported the retrieval
account without carrying that account's **own** scope line: that it does not show anyone held a
doctrine of individual bodily resurrection and does not date any doctrine. As written it read as
settling more than its source claimed. The disclaimer is now stated on the chapter's face, together
with the adjacent development account that occupies the space the disclaimer vacates, and the
methodological point on which both converge. **The fault was found by a hard refresh of the source
corpus rather than by internal review**, which is recorded because it says something about which
checks catch what.

**And an extraction fault in this work's own preparation.** The first pass at extracting the
office material from the theology codex indexed only one header level and silently missed
seventy-nine of one hundred and four coordinates, including the two most directly relevant. The
fault was found by checking the count against the register's own census, and it is recorded because
a study that reports others' selection effects should report its own.

---

## APPENDIX I · THE READING RULES, GATHERED

The four rules stated in the prefatory note, with the places they did work.

**The scriptural spine is read first and is the only seal-eligible register.** Applied throughout.
Most visibly at Chapter 89's seal census, where the finding is read off eight occurrences of one
root sorted by grammatical form, with the five blocking uses reported rather than suppressed.

**Interpretive traditions are witnesses, never authorities.** Applied evenhandedly to Sunni and
Shiʿi, Rabbanite and Karaite, Chalcedonian and Miaphysite and Nestorian, and to this work's own
sources. Chapter 84 runs the audit on this study.

**Claims carry their grade and never exceed it.** The tiers are in Appendix C. The places this
work said *this is a reading and not a corpus's word* are Chapters 3, 9, 14, 29, 30, 33, 69, 79, 83,
92, 97, and the excursus that repeat it.

**Consensus is not evidence.** Applied in both directions. The majority reading gets no deference
and the minority reading gets no glamour. The rule's sharpest application is Chapter 51's four
hundred prophets: one payer, one answer, counted once.

**And the reading rule that emerged from the material rather than being brought to it**: where the
corpus holds two things, this work holds two things. Ten pairs in the Hebrew material, an
unfurnished organ beside a declined offer, a covenant beside its complaint, a prologue beside a
comparative, an exaltation beside a limit.

**The corpora do not reconcile them. Neither does this.**


---

## APPENDIX J · THE CITATION STANDARD

The conventions this work uses, stated once so a reader can check any claim without guessing at
a reference.

### Scripture

**Hebrew Bible.** Book, chapter, verse, in the Hebrew versification where it differs from the
English. Malachi's forward unit is cited at 3:23-24, which is 4:5-6 in most English Bibles, and
the difference is noted at the place it arises.

**Gospels.** Book, chapter, verse. Where the earliest Gospel is meant and the context has not
already fixed it, it is named.

**Qurʾān.** Q sūra:āya. Where a sūra is named rather than numbered, the number follows on first
occurrence.

**Second Temple and pseudepigraphal material.** Standard sigla: 1QS, 11Q13, 4Q521, 4Q285 for the
Qumran texts, and book-and-chapter for 1 Enoch.

### Transliteration

**Hebrew and Aramaic** in a light academic transliteration marking the gutturals and emphatics
where they carry the argument, with *ʾ* for aleph and *ʿ* for ayin. Vowels are given as the
Masoretic pointing supplies them.

**Arabic** in a light academic transliteration with long vowels marked, *ʾ* for hamza and *ʿ* for
ayin, and the emphatics marked with a subscript dot.

**Greek** in Latin transliteration with long vowels marked *ē* and *ō*.

Transliteration is used throughout in place of original script, so that every philological claim
is legible to a reader who does not read the script and checkable by one who does.

### Original-language forms

Where a root, lemma, or construction carries a claim, it is given in transliteration in italic
with a gloss, and the claim is stated as a claim about that form. A reader who checks the form and
finds the claim does not hold has grounds to reject the finding, and that is the intended standing
of every philological statement in this work.

### Honorifics

Allah ﷻ, the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, and the prophets AS carry their honorifics in every citation,
gloss, and summary, including inside quoted verse. They are components of the names and not
optional additions, and a secular register does not license stripping them.

### Cross-references

Internal references are to chapters by number: *Chapter 34* means this work's thirty-fourth
chapter, not a chapter of any cited book. Book references are to this work's twelve books by
Roman numeral.

### What is not cited, and why

**No modern scholar is cited by name in the twelve books**, and this is deliberate rather than an
oversight.

Where a scholarly position bears on the argument, the **position** is named and this work's
relation to it is stated: Chapter 35 does this for three positions on messianic development,
monarchic source division, and canonical-critical reading. What is not done is to attach a
position to a name and then argue with the name.

The reason is Chapter 8's rule. Consensus is a fact about its holders and appears in these pages
as reception history rather than as warrant. A citation to an authority, used as support, is that
rule being broken; a citation to an authority, used to locate a position, is not. This work uses
the second and avoids the first, and the cost is that a reader wanting the literature must go to
the works listed in Appendix K rather than to footnotes here.

---

## APPENDIX K · BIBLIOGRAPHY

Every source this work draws on, sorted by what it is and by what it was used for. The sort is by
**function** rather than alphabetically, because a reader needs to know which sources carry
weight and which do not, and an alphabetical list conceals exactly that.

### I · PRIMARY SCRIPTURAL CORPORA

The four spines. Each is seal-eligible under Chapter 8's rule and each is read internally.

**The Hebrew Bible.** Masoretic text. Books cited: Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers,
Deuteronomy, Joshua, Judges, Ruth, 1 and 2 Samuel, 1 and 2 Kings, 1 and 2 Chronicles, Job, Psalms,
Proverbs, Isaiah, Jeremiah, Lamentations, Ezekiel, Daniel, Hosea, Amos, Micah, Habakkuk, Haggai,
Zechariah, Malachi.

**The Gospel according to Mark.** Greek text. Read entire, chapter by chapter, in Book VIII and
Book IX, in the oldest witnesses' ending at 16:8.

**The Gospel according to John.** Greek text. Read for the beloved-disciple designation at
13:23, 18:15-16, 19:26, 19:35, 20:2, 21:7, 21:20, 21:24, and for the prologue and the
subordination material at 5:19, 5:30, 10:30, 14:28, 17:3.

**Matthew and Luke** where they preserve a reading against Mark, and where the editorial direction
between them is the evidence, as at Mark 6:5 against Matthew 13:58.

**The Qurʾān.** Arabic text. Forty-seven sūras cited across one hundred and fourteen citations. The
principal loci: Q 2:117, 2:186, 2:213, 2:255; Q 3:42-55, 3:105, 3:144; Q 4:157-158, 4:171-172; Q
5:82, 5:110, 5:117; Q 8:17; Q 10:62; Q 13:28; Q 17:1, 17:74, 17:79; Q 18:110; Q 19:14-33; Q 20:121-122;
Q 22:37; Q 28:88; Q 29:51; Q 30:32; Q 33:7, 33:40, 33:56; Q 34:28; Q 37:123-132; Q 38:35; Q 42:9,
42:13, 42:24; Q 43:61; Q 44:54; Q 46:9; Q 47:19; Q 48:10; Q 50:38; Q 53:32; Q 55:26-27; Q 57:3,
57:27; Q 58:1, 58:7; Q 61:6; Q 66:12; Q 80:1-3; Q 83:25-26; Q 91:7; Q 110:3; Q 113 and 114.

### II · SECOND TEMPLE AND PSEUDEPIGRAPHAL MATERIAL

Read as evidence about the communities that produced it, never as a spine.

**1 Enoch**, the Parables, chapters 37 through 71, with the identification at chapter 71. Dating
contested; nothing in this work rests on its priority to any other corpus.

**1QS**, the Community Rule, at 9:11, for the plural anointed.

**The Damascus Document** and **1QSa**, for the same two-office structure.

**11Q13**, the Melchizedek text, for a heavenly deliverer constructed on four canonical verses.

**4Q521**, for the works that accompany the anointed one's coming, with the figure unnamed.

**4Q285**, the single damaged fragment whose broken line reads either way, recorded as the
candidate that does not decide.

**The Nag Hammadi corpus** and the systems the heresiologists describe, admitted as reception and
never as witness, under the Provenance Firewall.

### III · EPIGRAPHIC AND ARCHAEOLOGICAL WITNESSES

The documentary anchors. Each carries its own date and provenance and none is inferred.

**The Merneptah stele**, cut around 1208 BCE, Cairo, for the Canaan campaign line naming Israel
under the determinative for a people.

**The Ugaritic tablets**, Ras Shamra, excavated from 1929, for the portrait of El and for the Baal
cycle's drought sequence.

**Kuntillet Ajrud**, northeast Sinai, excavated 1975-76, dated around 800 BCE, for the storage-jar
inscriptions invoking blessing by the deity of Samaria and of Teman with the accompanying term.

**Khirbet el-Qom**, Judean hill country, eighth century, for the burial inscription carrying the
same attachment in a second region and a different genre.

**The Mari archive**, eighteenth century BCE, for court prophets with filed reports, credentials
checked by a lock of hair and a hem, and maintenance carried as a palace expense.

**Seal impressions and inscriptions across the Iron Age Levant** attesting the household office
*ʿal ha-bayit*, with no attestation for the individual holder named in the narrative.

### IV · LATER AUTHORITIES NAMED, NOT CITED AS WARRANT

Named because a historical event turns on them. None supplies warrant for any finding.

**Ahmad ibn Hanbal**, flogged and imprisoned from 833 for refusing the state examination in the
**Miḥna**, cited for the political economy of a doctrinal position.

**Maimonides**, for the creedal article of incorporeality, and **Rabad of Posquières**, for the
protest recorded at the moment of hardening.

**Middle Platonist and Aristotelian** categories, named as the source of terms absent from a
corpus's own founding documents.

### V · HISTORICAL CLAIMANTS

Recorded in Chapter 98 and Appendix G as a falsification corpus. Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya, late
seventh century; Ibn Tūmart and the Almohad founding, twelfth; Sayyid Muḥammad Jaunpūrī, late
fifteenth; Muḥammad Aḥmad in the Sudan, 1880s; the Bābī claimant, nineteenth-century Persia;
Muḥammad bin ʿAbd Allāh al-Qaḥṭānī, 1979.

### VI · SCHOLARLY POSITIONS ENGAGED

Named as positions, per Appendix J's convention. This work states its relation to each and argues
with none by name.

**Messianism as a comparatively late development**, not present in the pre-exilic royal texts.
Mowinckel and successors. *Compatible*; this work commits to no dating beyond the corpus's own
narrative order.

**The monarchy tension resolved by source division** into pro- and anti-monarchic strands.
Classical source criticism on 1 Samuel 8 to 12, and the Deuteronomistic History hypothesis. *No
position taken*; the final form's retention of both is the datum.

**The royal and future-royal texts as the interpretive centre.** Much confessional and
canonical-critical reading. *Directly opposed at the sorting*, though not on the reality or
importance of the material.

**Second Temple apocalyptic as authored under crisis.** *Opposed at the mechanism*, per Chapter
82: the account asserts a development without supplying one, and its own best observation
contradicts its conclusion.

**The beloved-disciple anonymity as concealment.** *Reframed*, per Chapter 75: the alternative is
that a position is specified rather than a name withheld.

**The sabbath literature.** *Adjacent*. This work's interest is in the terminus-word rather than
in observance, and connects the two only through the root-field.

### VII · THE SOURCE STUDIES

Prior work by the same hand, on which this work's readings of particular corpora rest. Each was
itself built scripture-internally and each is superseded by nothing here.

*A Shoot from a Felled Stump: The Conceded Throne.* The terminus, the concession engine, the
canonical ladder, the removability test, and the custody transfers. Books II, III, IV, and V.

*The Clean Gospel of Mark: Passage Commentary from Scripture.* The Markan spine and the distilled
Qurʾānic christology. Books VIII and IX.

*Mercy of Light: Integrated Sira.* Book XI.

*Servant and Seal.* The three registers, the seal census, and the trials. Book XI.

*Difficult Passages of the Qurʾān and the Text's Own Instructions for Reading Them.* Governs every
Qurʾānic citation in this work.

*The Custody Cycle of the Most High.* The seven stations, the two manufactures, and the
capture distinction. Book X.

*The Apocalypse Was Not an Invention but a Retrieval.* Chapter 82.

*The Disciple Whom Jesus Loved.* Chapter 75.

*The Divine Feminine: A Structural and Comparative Account of the Receptive Pole.* Chapter 83.

*Ilyas AS: The Life Assembled from the Text Alone*, and *Ilyas AS: The Office of Unfunded Speech.*
Book VII.

*ʿĪsā AS: The Life the Qurʾān Will Support.* Books VIII and IX.

*Qurʾānic Stations: A Root-Geometric Reading.* Governs root excavation throughout.

*The Birth of Heaven and Hell.* The comparative history of the accountable soul, the five
renovations, and the 4Q521 formula returned as an answer about identity. Chapters 46, 57, and 82.

*The Two Waters.* The *s-k-n* root, the dwelling-verb at Q 23:18, and the awaited-against-secured
distinction marked at a preposition. Chapter 10.

*On the Ninth Gate.* The severance as a real membrane, the impossibility of a crossing native to
the bounded side, and the aperture granted rather than seized. Chapter 96.

*Freedom as the Fibre of an Invariant Map.* The measured aperture width, the per-instrument
accounting, and the licence bounded to direction and issued per run. Chapter 102.

### VIII · THE INSTRUMENT DEPENDENCY, DECLARED

Chapter 84's audit found this work's debt partially outstanding, and this section is where the
outstanding part is itemised rather than left as a general admission.

**Concordances, lexica, and grammars** for Hebrew, Aramaic, Greek, and Arabic were used throughout
and are indispensable to every philological claim in this work.

**Critical editions and apparatus** for the Hebrew, Greek, and Qurʾānic texts, and for the Qumran
sigla.

**Standard reference works** for the epigraphic and archaeological data in section III, including
the excavation reports for the sites named.

These are products of exactly the scholarly traditions Chapter 8's firewall holds at arm's length,
and **there is no version of this study that does without them.**

The firewall was never a claim that those traditions produced nothing reliable. It is a claim
about what may **settle** a question, and the instruments do not settle questions; they make the
text legible so that the text can. That distinction is what makes the dependency consistent with
the rule rather than an exception to it, and a reader who finds the distinction too convenient has
this work's own statement, at Chapter 84, that the debt is partially paid and partially
outstanding.

### IX · WHAT IS ABSENT

Declared here as well as at Appendix D, because a bibliography is where a reader looks for what a
work read.

**No primary rabbinic corpus.** No Mishnah, Talmud, Midrash, or Targum was in hand.

**No primary patristic or conciliar corpus.** The settlement's documents are described in Book X
and are not quoted as primary.

**No primary Shiʿi corpus.** No Shiʿi hadith collection or Imami source was in hand.

Every claim resting on these three is reported at secondary tier and adjudicated nowhere, and no
argument in the twelve books turns on one.
